Like, I’ll tell myself, “Just sit still, it’s not that hard,” and then ten minutes later I’m standing up to refill my water, or I’m walking over by the printer, or kind of pacing for a second and pretending I’m checking something. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. I just feel like I have to move, and then afterward I worry people notice or think I’m not being professional. That’s really what made me think maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling the need to move or fidget in situations outside of work, like when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the clearest example is definitely work, especially those long sales strategy meetings. That’s honestly the thing that made me come in, because I know I’m supposed to just stay seated like everyone else, and I’ll start out trying to do that, but then I’ll get this urge to get up.\n\nSometimes I make it look normal, like I’m just refilling my water or going over by the printer, but really I’m just needing to move for a second. I’ll even pace a little, like I’m checking something, and then sit back down and hope nobody thought it was weird. So outside of work I guess I’d have to think about it more, but the meeting thing is the one that really stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you have the urge to move or fidget?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think restless is probably the right word. It’s not always like I’m consciously thinking, “I need to move right now,” but in those long sales strategy meetings it builds up in this really uncomfortable way. I’ll be sitting there knowing I’m supposed to stay in my seat, trying to look like I’m listening and being normal, and then I just get this strong urge to get up.\n\nSo I’ll do something that seems acceptable, like refill my water even if I don’t really need more water, or I’ll walk over near the printer, or sort of pace for a second like I’m checking on something. It’s honestly the main reason I came in, because I know everyone else can just sit through the meeting, and I keep leaving my seat even though I’m trying not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what's being discussed during those long sales strategy meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ll go in thinking, okay, I’m going to pay attention, I’m going to follow the numbers, I’m going to track what the plan is for this client or campaign. And for the first few minutes I usually can. But then at some point I sort of… drift, I guess.\n\nLike I’ll be looking right at the slide on the screen, or at the brief in front of me, and suddenly realize I’ve been staring at the same chart or the same paragraph and I have no idea what someone just said. It’s not that I don’t care, because I do care, and I know it matters for my job. But it’s like my brain slips off the topic without me noticing, and then I’m trying to piece together the last point from context so I don’t have to ask someone to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you have the urge to move or fidget during those meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, there’s definitely a kind of tension or discomfort when I’m sitting there and I feel like I need to move, but it doesn’t always feel like worry in the classic sense.\n\nSometimes I do get a little self-conscious about it, like I’ll think, “Is everyone noticing that I keep shifting around?” or “Does it look unprofessional if I get up again?” But the main feeling is more like being trapped in my chair, if that makes sense. Like my body is saying I need to get up for a second, and then my mind starts trying to find a socially acceptable excuse to do it.\n\nSo… anxious maybe a little, but I’m not sure that’s the main word I’d use. Restless or uncomfortable feels more accurate.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or the need to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. When I look back, it was definitely there way before high school. I remember elementary school being kind of like… I was not a “bad” kid exactly, and I did well enough that people would say I was bright, but the comments from teachers were always these patterns.\n\nThey’d write things like I rushed through work, or I talked out of turn, or I needed to slow down and check my answers. And homework was a whole thing — I’d forget it, or I’d do part of it and then it would disappear into my backpack or my desk. My desk at school was honestly kind of a disaster, just stuffed with half-finished papers and worksheets and things I meant to finish or turn in.\n\nSo, yes, I do remember it starting pretty early. At the time I think everyone just framed it as me needing to try harder or be more organized, but looking back, that restlessness and rushing and not staying on track were already there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying on track consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were less noticeable?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been kind of there in the background for a long time, but if you’re asking when it became noticeably affecting me in a way I couldn’t just brush off, that’s been more since I moved into advertising sales strategy — so about two years and two months ago.\n\nBefore that, I could sort of compensate, or it wasn’t as obvious day to day. But in this role, with the longer strategy discussions and needing to track details over time, it became much harder to ignore. It’s not like it suddenly appeared out of nowhere, but that was when it started feeling like, okay, this is actually getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had relationship problems, or experienced other significant difficulties because of your restlessness or inability to stay on track?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job, no. And I wouldn’t say it’s caused some huge relationship blowup or anything like that. But at work, yes, it’s starting to matter in a way that scares me a little.\n\nIt’s shown up in performance reviews, not like “you’re failing,” but more comments about needing to be more consistent with follow-through, staying on top of details, not letting things slip between conversations and next steps. And in my job, client trust is a big deal, so when I miss something or seem scattered, it’s not just embarrassing, it can actually make the client wonder if I’m on top of their account.\n\nThere was one client complaint in particular that really got to me. I cared a lot about doing good work for them, and I still do, but something about how I handled the tracking or follow-up made them feel like they couldn’t fully rely on me. I remember thinking, “Okay, this isn’t just me being quirky or disorganized in private. This could actually damage an account.”\n\nSo, not catastrophic, but significant enough that I’m worried. It feels like the gap between how much I care and how consistently I execute is becoming visible to other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying on track is more because you have trouble paying attention or because you get distracted by the urge to move or do something else?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention part, honestly. The movement or wanting to do something else can be there sometimes, but the thing I notice most is that my brain just… slips off the track.\n\nLike in longer strategy meetings, I’ll start out really engaged. I’ll be looking at the deck, taking in what someone is saying, and I genuinely intend to follow the whole conversation. But then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same slide and I don’t actually know what the last point was. It’s not always that I’m physically getting up or needing to move right then. It’s more like I’m present in the room, but I’m not absorbing it.\n\nSame thing when I’m reading a client brief. I can begin focused, and then I’ll catch myself on the same paragraph or the same sentence, and I have no idea what I just read. Sometimes I’ll have to go back and reread it, and even then I’m sort of fighting to keep my attention anchored there.\n\nSo, if I had to choose, I’d say it’s more trouble sustaining attention than being distracted specifically by the urge to move. It feels like my focus just kind of fades out without me noticing until I’ve already missed something.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your restlessness or difficulty staying on track?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I’m sure there are little moments where someone close to me has been annoyed because I seemed distracted or I forgot part of a conversation, but I wouldn’t say it’s caused serious relationship problems.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve had friendships end or a partner say, “This is unbearable,” at least not because of that. If anything, it’s more that I sometimes worry I come across as not fully present, even when I really do care. Like I’ll be listening, and then later realize I didn’t retain as much as I should have, and then I feel bad because I don’t want someone to think I’m dismissing them.\n\nBut overall, no, I don’t think my personal relationships have taken a big hit from it. It feels much more obvious and consequential at work than in my personal life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying on track consistently since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I don’t have a perfect memory of being a little kid, obviously, but this doesn’t feel like something that suddenly started in adulthood.\n\nI remember it being there way before high school. In elementary school, my teachers would write things like I was bright, but I rushed through work, or I talked out of turn, or I forgot to bring in homework even when I had done it. And my desk was always kind of a disaster — like stuffed with half-finished papers and old worksheets and things I meant to finish or turn in but somehow didn’t.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone framed it as a serious issue, because I did well enough academically and I was considered capable. But looking back, the pattern feels really familiar: I’d understand things, I’d want to do well, but the follow-through and keeping track of everything were already messy. So yes, it feels like this has been pretty consistent since childhood, just showing up in different ways now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness or difficulty staying on track has significantly impacted your ability to achieve your goals or maintain a certain standard in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s where it’s started to feel more serious for me. For a long time I could kind of compensate, or at least I thought I could. I’d put in extra time, double-check things, try to make myself care enough that it would override the difficulty staying on track. And because I do care a lot about doing good work, I think I told myself it wasn’t really affecting my goals that much.\n\nBut at work, it has started showing up in ways that are harder to brush off. It’s come up in performance reviews — not necessarily as, like, “you’re terrible at your job,” but more in terms of consistency, follow-through, being fully on top of details, staying aligned with client expectations. And that’s been really upsetting because I’m in advertising sales, so so much of the job is about people trusting that I’m listening, that I understand what they need, that I’ll follow through.\n\nThere was one client complaint in particular that really shook me. I don’t want to overstate it like I ruined the whole account, but it made me worry that if I miss something or don’t stay on top of the thread of what’s happening, I could actually damage client relationships or accounts. And that’s scary, because it’s not that I don’t care. If anything, I care a lot, and then I feel awful when the outcome doesn’t match the effort I thought I was putting in.\n\nSo yes, professionally especially, I do feel like it’s started to affect my ability to maintain the standard I want for myself. Maybe mildly in the sense that I’m still functioning and still working, but it’s noticeable enough now that I can’t just write it off as being busy or having an off week.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F90.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I mean, I’m sure there are little moments where someone close to me has been annoyed because I seemed distracted or I forgot part of a conversation, but I wouldn’t say it’s caused serious relationship problems.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve had friendships end or a partner say, “This is unbearable,” at least not because of that. If anything, it’s more that I sometimes worry I come across as not fully present, even when I really do care. Like I’ll be listening, and then later realize I didn’t retain as much as I should have, and then I feel bad because I don’t want someone to think I’m dismissing them.\n\nBut overall, no, I don’t think my personal relationships have taken a big hit from it. It feels much more obvious and consequential at work than in my personal life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying on track consistently since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I don’t have a perfect memory of being a little kid, obviously, but this doesn’t feel like something that suddenly started in adulthood.\n\nI remember it being there way before high school. In elementary school, my teachers would write things like I was bright, but I rushed through work, or I talked out of turn, or I forgot to bring in homework even when I had done it. And my desk was always kind of a disaster — like stuffed with half-finished papers and old worksheets and things I meant to finish or turn in but somehow didn’t.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone framed it as a serious issue, because I did well enough academically and I was considered capable. But looking back, the pattern feels really familiar: I’d understand things, I’d want to do well, but the follow-through and keeping track of everything were already messy. So yes, it feels like this has been pretty consistent since childhood, just showing up in different ways now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness or difficulty staying on track has significantly impacted your ability to achieve your goals or maintain a certain standard in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s where it’s started to feel more serious for me. For a long time I could kind of compensate, or at least I thought I could. I’d put in extra time, double-check things, try to make myself care enough that it would override the difficulty staying on track. And because I do care a lot about doing good work, I think I told myself it wasn’t really affecting my goals that much.\n\nBut at work, it has started showing up in ways that are harder to brush off. It’s come up in performance reviews — not necessarily as, like, “you’re terrible at your job,” but more in terms of consistency, follow-through, being fully on top of details, staying aligned with client expectations. And that’s been really upsetting because I’m in advertising sales, so so much of the job is about people trusting that I’m listening, that I understand what they need, that I’ll follow through.\n\nThere was one client complaint in particular that really shook me. I don’t want to overstate it like I ruined the whole account, but it made me worry that if I miss something or don’t stay on top of the thread of what’s happening, I could actually damage client relationships or accounts. And that’s scary, because it’s not that I don’t care. If anything, I care a lot, and then I feel awful when the outcome doesn’t match the effort I thought I was putting in.\n\nSo yes, professionally especially, I do feel like it’s started to affect my ability to maintain the standard I want for myself. Maybe mildly in the sense that I’m still functioning and still working, but it’s noticeable enough now that I can’t just write it off as being busy or having an off week.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any significant problems in your social relationships because of your restlessness or difficulty staying on track?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no — at least not anything I’d call significant.

I mean, I can think of small things. Like, if I’m with friends and my mind drifts for a second, I might miss part of what someone said, or I might interrupt without meaning to because I’m excited or I think I’ll forget what I was going to say. And sometimes I’ll feel embarrassed afterward, like, “Was I being rude?” But I don’t think it has caused major social problems.

I still have friends, I’m still invited places, and I don’t think people are avoiding me because of it. If anything, I’m probably more worried about it internally than other people are reacting to it outwardly. So no, I wouldn’t say it’s significantly damaged my social relationships.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is this thing that keeps happening at work, especially in these long sales strategy meetings. I’m in advertising sales, so we have these meetings where everyone’s supposed to sit, listen, go through numbers and plans and client stuff, and I know I’m supposed to just stay in my chair like everyone else.\n\nBut I keep getting up. Like, I’ll tell myself, “Just sit still, it’s not that hard,” and then ten minutes later I’m standing up to refill my water, or I’m walking over by the printer, or kind of pacing for a second and pretending I’m checking something. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive. I just feel like I have to move, and then afterward I worry people notice or think I’m not being professional. That’s really what made me think maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling the need to move or fidget in situations outside of work, like when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the clearest example is definitely work, especially those long sales strategy meetings. That’s honestly the thing that made me come in, because I know I’m supposed to just stay seated like everyone else, and I’ll start out trying to do that, but then I’ll get this urge to get up.\n\nSometimes I make it look normal, like I’m just refilling my water or going over by the printer, but really I’m just needing to move for a second. I’ll even pace a little, like I’m checking something, and then sit back down and hope nobody thought it was weird. So outside of work I guess I’d have to think about it more, but the meeting thing is the one that really stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you have the urge to move or fidget?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think restless is probably the right word. It’s not always like I’m consciously thinking, “I need to move right now,” but in those long sales strategy meetings it builds up in this really uncomfortable way. I’ll be sitting there knowing I’m supposed to stay in my seat, trying to look like I’m listening and being normal, and then I just get this strong urge to get up.\n\nSo I’ll do something that seems acceptable, like refill my water even if I don’t really need more water, or I’ll walk over near the printer, or sort of pace for a second like I’m checking on something. It’s honestly the main reason I came in, because I know everyone else can just sit through the meeting, and I keep leaving my seat even though I’m trying not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what's being discussed during those long sales strategy meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ll go in thinking, okay, I’m going to pay attention, I’m going to follow the numbers, I’m going to track what the plan is for this client or campaign. And for the first few minutes I usually can. But then at some point I sort of… drift, I guess.\n\nLike I’ll be looking right at the slide on the screen, or at the brief in front of me, and suddenly realize I’ve been staring at the same chart or the same paragraph and I have no idea what someone just said. It’s not that I don’t care, because I do care, and I know it matters for my job. But it’s like my brain slips off the topic without me noticing, and then I’m trying to piece together the last point from context so I don’t have to ask someone to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you have the urge to move or fidget during those meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, there’s definitely a kind of tension or discomfort when I’m sitting there and I feel like I need to move, but it doesn’t always feel like worry in the classic sense.\n\nSometimes I do get a little self-conscious about it, like I’ll think, “Is everyone noticing that I keep shifting around?” or “Does it look unprofessional if I get up again?” But the main feeling is more like being trapped in my chair, if that makes sense. Like my body is saying I need to get up for a second, and then my mind starts trying to find a socially acceptable excuse to do it.\n\nSo… anxious maybe a little, but I’m not sure that’s the main word I’d use. Restless or uncomfortable feels more accurate.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or the need to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. When I look back, it was definitely there way before high school. I remember elementary school being kind of like… I was not a “bad” kid exactly, and I did well enough that people would say I was bright, but the comments from teachers were always these patterns.\n\nThey’d write things like I rushed through work, or I talked out of turn, or I needed to slow down and check my answers. And homework was a whole thing — I’d forget it, or I’d do part of it and then it would disappear into my backpack or my desk. My desk at school was honestly kind of a disaster, just stuffed with half-finished papers and worksheets and things I meant to finish or turn in.\n\nSo, yes, I do remember it starting pretty early. At the time I think everyone just framed it as me needing to try harder or be more organized, but looking back, that restlessness and rushing and not staying on track were already there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying on track consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were less noticeable?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been kind of there in the background for a long time, but if you’re asking when it became noticeably affecting me in a way I couldn’t just brush off, that’s been more since I moved into advertising sales strategy — so about two years and two months ago.\n\nBefore that, I could sort of compensate, or it wasn’t as obvious day to day. But in this role, with the longer strategy discussions and needing to track details over time, it became much harder to ignore. It’s not like it suddenly appeared out of nowhere, but that was when it started feeling like, okay, this is actually getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had relationship problems, or experienced other significant difficulties because of your restlessness or inability to stay on track?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job, no. And I wouldn’t say it’s caused some huge relationship blowup or anything like that. But at work, yes, it’s starting to matter in a way that scares me a little.\n\nIt’s shown up in performance reviews, not like “you’re failing,” but more comments about needing to be more consistent with follow-through, staying on top of details, not letting things slip between conversations and next steps. And in my job, client trust is a big deal, so when I miss something or seem scattered, it’s not just embarrassing, it can actually make the client wonder if I’m on top of their account.\n\nThere was one client complaint in particular that really got to me. I cared a lot about doing good work for them, and I still do, but something about how I handled the tracking or follow-up made them feel like they couldn’t fully rely on me. I remember thinking, “Okay, this isn’t just me being quirky or disorganized in private. This could actually damage an account.”\n\nSo, not catastrophic, but significant enough that I’m worried. It feels like the gap between how much I care and how consistently I execute is becoming visible to other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying on track is more because you have trouble paying attention or because you get distracted by the urge to move or do something else?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention part, honestly. The movement or wanting to do something else can be there sometimes, but the thing I notice most is that my brain just… slips off the track.\n\nLike in longer strategy meetings, I’ll start out really engaged. I’ll be looking at the deck, taking in what someone is saying, and I genuinely intend to follow the whole conversation. But then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same slide and I don’t actually know what the last point was. It’s not always that I’m physically getting up or needing to move right then. It’s more like I’m present in the room, but I’m not absorbing it.\n\nSame thing when I’m reading a client brief. I can begin focused, and then I’ll catch myself on the same paragraph or the same sentence, and I have no idea what I just read. Sometimes I’ll have to go back and reread it, and even then I’m sort of fighting to keep my attention anchored there.\n\nSo, if I had to choose, I’d say it’s more trouble sustaining attention than being distracted specifically by the urge to move. It feels like my focus just kind of fades out without me noticing until I’ve already missed something.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your restlessness or difficulty staying on track?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. But it’s like my brain slips off the topic without me noticing, and then I’m trying to piece together the last point from context so I don’t have to ask someone to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you have the urge to move or fidget during those meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, there’s definitely a kind of tension or discomfort when I’m sitting there and I feel like I need to move, but it doesn’t always feel like worry in the classic sense.\n\nSometimes I do get a little self-conscious about it, like I’ll think, “Is everyone noticing that I keep shifting around?” or “Does it look unprofessional if I get up again?” But the main feeling is more like being trapped in my chair, if that makes sense. Like my body is saying I need to get up for a second, and then my mind starts trying to find a socially acceptable excuse to do it.\n\nSo… anxious maybe a little, but I’m not sure that’s the main word I’d use. Restless or uncomfortable feels more accurate.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or the need to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. When I look back, it was definitely there way before high school. I remember elementary school being kind of like… I was not a “bad” kid exactly, and I did well enough that people would say I was bright, but the comments from teachers were always these patterns.\n\nThey’d write things like I rushed through work, or I talked out of turn, or I needed to slow down and check my answers. And homework was a whole thing — I’d forget it, or I’d do part of it and then it would disappear into my backpack or my desk. My desk at school was honestly kind of a disaster, just stuffed with half-finished papers and worksheets and things I meant to finish or turn in.\n\nSo, yes, I do remember it starting pretty early. At the time I think everyone just framed it as me needing to try harder or be more organized, but looking back, that restlessness and rushing and not staying on track were already there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying on track consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were less noticeable?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been kind of there in the background for a long time, but if you’re asking when it became noticeably affecting me in a way I couldn’t just brush off, that’s been more since I moved into advertising sales strategy — so about two years and two months ago.\n\nBefore that, I could sort of compensate, or it wasn’t as obvious day to day. But in this role, with the longer strategy discussions and needing to track details over time, it became much harder to ignore. It’s not like it suddenly appeared out of nowhere, but that was when it started feeling like, okay, this is actually getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had relationship problems, or experienced other significant difficulties because of your restlessness or inability to stay on track?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job, no. And I wouldn’t say it’s caused some huge relationship blowup or anything like that. But at work, yes, it’s starting to matter in a way that scares me a little.\n\nIt’s shown up in performance reviews, not like “you’re failing,” but more comments about needing to be more consistent with follow-through, staying on top of details, not letting things slip between conversations and next steps. And in my job, client trust is a big deal, so when I miss something or seem scattered, it’s not just embarrassing, it can actually make the client wonder if I’m on top of their account.\n\nThere was one client complaint in particular that really got to me. I cared a lot about doing good work for them, and I still do, but something about how I handled the tracking or follow-up made them feel like they couldn’t fully rely on me. I remember thinking, “Okay, this isn’t just me being quirky or disorganized in private. This could actually damage an account.”\n\nSo, not catastrophic, but significant enough that I’m worried. It feels like the gap between how much I care and how consistently I execute is becoming visible to other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying on track is more because you have trouble paying attention or because you get distracted by the urge to move or do something else?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention part, honestly. The movement or wanting to do something else can be there sometimes, but the thing I notice most is that my brain just… slips off the track.\n\nLike in longer strategy meetings, I’ll start out really engaged. I’ll be looking at the deck, taking in what someone is saying, and I genuinely intend to follow the whole conversation. But then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same slide and I don’t actually know what the last point was. It’s not always that I’m physically getting up or needing to move right then. It’s more like I’m present in the room, but I’m not absorbing it.\n\nSame thing when I’m reading a client brief. I can begin focused, and then I’ll catch myself on the same paragraph or the same sentence, and I have no idea what I just read. Sometimes I’ll have to go back and reread it, and even then I’m sort of fighting to keep my attention anchored there.\n\nSo, if I had to choose, I’d say it’s more trouble sustaining attention than being distracted specifically by the urge to move. It feels like my focus just kind of fades out without me noticing until I’ve already missed something.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your restlessness or difficulty staying on track?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I’m sure there are little moments where someone close to me has been annoyed because I seemed distracted or I forgot part of a conversation, but I wouldn’t say it’s caused serious relationship problems.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve had friendships end or a partner say, “This is unbearable,” at least not because of that. If anything, it’s more that I sometimes worry I come across as not fully present, even when I really do care. Like I’ll be listening, and then later realize I didn’t retain as much as I should have, and then I feel bad because I don’t want someone to think I’m dismissing them.\n\nBut overall, no, I don’t think my personal relationships have taken a big hit from it. It feels much more obvious and consequential at work than in my personal life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying on track consistently since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I don’t have a perfect memory of being a little kid, obviously, but this doesn’t feel like something that suddenly started in adulthood.\n\nI remember it being there way before high school. In elementary school, my teachers would write things like I was bright, but I rushed through work, or I talked out of turn, or I forgot to bring in homework even when I had done it. And my desk was always kind of a disaster — like stuffed with half-finished papers and old worksheets and things I meant to finish or turn in but somehow didn’t.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone framed it as a serious issue, because I did well enough academically and I was considered capable. But looking back, the pattern feels really familiar: I’d understand things, I’d want to do well, but the follow-through and keeping track of everything were already messy. So yes, it feels like this has been pretty consistent since childhood, just showing up in different ways now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness or difficulty staying on track has significantly impacted your ability to achieve your goals or maintain a certain standard in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s where it’s started to feel more serious for me. For a long time I could kind of compensate, or at least I thought I could. I’d put in extra time, double-check things, try to make myself care enough that it would override the difficulty staying on track. And because I do care a lot about doing good work, I think I told myself it wasn’t really affecting my goals that much.\n\nBut at work, it has started showing up in ways that are harder to brush off. It’s come up in performance reviews — not necessarily as, like, “you’re terrible at your job,” but more in terms of consistency, follow-through, being fully on top of details, staying aligned with client expectations. And that’s been really upsetting because I’m in advertising sales, so so much of the job is about people trusting that I’m listening, that I understand what they need, that I’ll follow through.\n\nThere was one client complaint in particular that really shook me. I don’t want to overstate it like I ruined the whole account, but it made me worry that if I miss something or don’t stay on top of the thread of what’s happening, I could actually damage client relationships or accounts. And that’s scary, because it’s not that I don’t care. If anything, I care a lot, and then I feel awful when the outcome doesn’t match the effort I thought I was putting in.\n\nSo yes, professionally especially, I do feel like it’s started to affect my ability to maintain the standard I want for myself. Maybe mildly in the sense that I’m still functioning and still working, but it’s noticeable enough now that I can’t just write it off as being busy or having an off week.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant problems in your social relationships because of your restlessness or difficulty staying on track?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not anything I’d call significant.\n\nI mean, I can think of small things. Like, if I’m with friends and my mind drifts for a second, I might miss part of what someone said, or I might interrupt without meaning to because I’m excited or I think I’ll forget what I was going to say. And sometimes I’ll feel embarrassed afterward, like, “Was I being rude?” But I don’t think it has caused major social problems.\n\nI still have friends, I’m still invited places, and I don’t think people are avoiding me because of it. If anything, I’m probably more worried about it internally than other people are reacting to it outwardly. So no, I wouldn’t say it’s significantly damaged my social relationships.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I do that more than I’d like to admit.

It happens a lot in work conversations, especially with clients or coworkers. Someone will still be asking the question, and I’ll already think I know where they’re going, so I jump in with an answer before they’ve actually finished. In my head it feels like I’m being responsive or helpful, like, “Oh, I know this one,” but then sometimes I realize halfway through that I answered the wrong thing because they were actually asking something slightly different.

And then I feel embarrassed because it can come across like I wasn’t listening, or like I cut them off. I’m not trying to be rude — it’s more like the answer is already forming and I almost feel this pressure to get it out before I lose it. But yes, I definitely interrupt or blurt things out sometimes, and then afterward I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just let them finish?”

So outside of work I guess I’d have to think about it more, but the meeting thing is the one that really stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you have the urge to move or fidget?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think restless is probably the right word. It’s not always like I’m consciously thinking, “I need to move right now,” but in those long sales strategy meetings it builds up in this really uncomfortable way. I’ll be sitting there knowing I’m supposed to stay in my seat, trying to look like I’m listening and being normal, and then I just get this strong urge to get up.\n\nSo I’ll do something that seems acceptable, like refill my water even if I don’t really need more water, or I’ll walk over near the printer, or sort of pace for a second like I’m checking on something. It’s honestly the main reason I came in, because I know everyone else can just sit through the meeting, and I keep leaving my seat even though I’m trying not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what's being discussed during those long sales strategy meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ll go in thinking, okay, I’m going to pay attention, I’m going to follow the numbers, I’m going to track what the plan is for this client or campaign. And for the first few minutes I usually can. But then at some point I sort of… drift, I guess.\n\nLike I’ll be looking right at the slide on the screen, or at the brief in front of me, and suddenly realize I’ve been staring at the same chart or the same paragraph and I have no idea what someone just said. It’s not that I don’t care, because I do care, and I know it matters for my job. But it’s like my brain slips off the topic without me noticing, and then I’m trying to piece together the last point from context so I don’t have to ask someone to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you have the urge to move or fidget during those meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, there’s definitely a kind of tension or discomfort when I’m sitting there and I feel like I need to move, but it doesn’t always feel like worry in the classic sense.\n\nSometimes I do get a little self-conscious about it, like I’ll think, “Is everyone noticing that I keep shifting around?” or “Does it look unprofessional if I get up again?” But the main feeling is more like being trapped in my chair, if that makes sense. Like my body is saying I need to get up for a second, and then my mind starts trying to find a socially acceptable excuse to do it.\n\nSo… anxious maybe a little, but I’m not sure that’s the main word I’d use. Restless or uncomfortable feels more accurate.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or the need to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. When I look back, it was definitely there way before high school. I remember elementary school being kind of like… I was not a “bad” kid exactly, and I did well enough that people would say I was bright, but the comments from teachers were always these patterns.\n\nThey’d write things like I rushed through work, or I talked out of turn, or I needed to slow down and check my answers. And homework was a whole thing — I’d forget it, or I’d do part of it and then it would disappear into my backpack or my desk. My desk at school was honestly kind of a disaster, just stuffed with half-finished papers and worksheets and things I meant to finish or turn in.\n\nSo, yes, I do remember it starting pretty early. At the time I think everyone just framed it as me needing to try harder or be more organized, but looking back, that restlessness and rushing and not staying on track were already there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying on track consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were less noticeable?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been kind of there in the background for a long time, but if you’re asking when it became noticeably affecting me in a way I couldn’t just brush off, that’s been more since I moved into advertising sales strategy — so about two years and two months ago.\n\nBefore that, I could sort of compensate, or it wasn’t as obvious day to day. But in this role, with the longer strategy discussions and needing to track details over time, it became much harder to ignore. It’s not like it suddenly appeared out of nowhere, but that was when it started feeling like, okay, this is actually getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had relationship problems, or experienced other significant difficulties because of your restlessness or inability to stay on track?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job, no. And I wouldn’t say it’s caused some huge relationship blowup or anything like that. But at work, yes, it’s starting to matter in a way that scares me a little.\n\nIt’s shown up in performance reviews, not like “you’re failing,” but more comments about needing to be more consistent with follow-through, staying on top of details, not letting things slip between conversations and next steps. And in my job, client trust is a big deal, so when I miss something or seem scattered, it’s not just embarrassing, it can actually make the client wonder if I’m on top of their account.\n\nThere was one client complaint in particular that really got to me. I cared a lot about doing good work for them, and I still do, but something about how I handled the tracking or follow-up made them feel like they couldn’t fully rely on me. I remember thinking, “Okay, this isn’t just me being quirky or disorganized in private. This could actually damage an account.”\n\nSo, not catastrophic, but significant enough that I’m worried. It feels like the gap between how much I care and how consistently I execute is becoming visible to other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying on track is more because you have trouble paying attention or because you get distracted by the urge to move or do something else?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention part, honestly. The movement or wanting to do something else can be there sometimes, but the thing I notice most is that my brain just… slips off the track.\n\nLike in longer strategy meetings, I’ll start out really engaged. I’ll be looking at the deck, taking in what someone is saying, and I genuinely intend to follow the whole conversation. But then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same slide and I don’t actually know what the last point was. It’s not always that I’m physically getting up or needing to move right then. It’s more like I’m present in the room, but I’m not absorbing it.\n\nSame thing when I’m reading a client brief. I can begin focused, and then I’ll catch myself on the same paragraph or the same sentence, and I have no idea what I just read. Sometimes I’ll have to go back and reread it, and even then I’m sort of fighting to keep my attention anchored there.\n\nSo, if I had to choose, I’d say it’s more trouble sustaining attention than being distracted specifically by the urge to move. It feels like my focus just kind of fades out without me noticing until I’ve already missed something.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your restlessness or difficulty staying on track?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I’m sure there are little moments where someone close to me has been annoyed because I seemed distracted or I forgot part of a conversation, but I wouldn’t say it’s caused serious relationship problems.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve had friendships end or a partner say, “This is unbearable,” at least not because of that. If anything, it’s more that I sometimes worry I come across as not fully present, even when I really do care. Like I’ll be listening, and then later realize I didn’t retain as much as I should have, and then I feel bad because I don’t want someone to think I’m dismissing them.\n\nBut overall, no, I don’t think my personal relationships have taken a big hit from it. It feels much more obvious and consequential at work than in my personal life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying on track consistently since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I don’t have a perfect memory of being a little kid, obviously, but this doesn’t feel like something that suddenly started in adulthood.\n\nI remember it being there way before high school. In elementary school, my teachers would write things like I was bright, but I rushed through work, or I talked out of turn, or I forgot to bring in homework even when I had done it. And my desk was always kind of a disaster — like stuffed with half-finished papers and old worksheets and things I meant to finish or turn in but somehow didn’t.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone framed it as a serious issue, because I did well enough academically and I was considered capable. But looking back, the pattern feels really familiar: I’d understand things, I’d want to do well, but the follow-through and keeping track of everything were already messy. So yes, it feels like this has been pretty consistent since childhood, just showing up in different ways now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness or difficulty staying on track has significantly impacted your ability to achieve your goals or maintain a certain standard in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s where it’s started to feel more serious for me. For a long time I could kind of compensate, or at least I thought I could. I’d put in extra time, double-check things, try to make myself care enough that it would override the difficulty staying on track. And because I do care a lot about doing good work, I think I told myself it wasn’t really affecting my goals that much.\n\nBut at work, it has started showing up in ways that are harder to brush off. It’s come up in performance reviews — not necessarily as, like, “you’re terrible at your job,” but more in terms of consistency, follow-through, being fully on top of details, staying aligned with client expectations. And that’s been really upsetting because I’m in advertising sales, so so much of the job is about people trusting that I’m listening, that I understand what they need, that I’ll follow through.\n\nThere was one client complaint in particular that really shook me. I don’t want to overstate it like I ruined the whole account, but it made me worry that if I miss something or don’t stay on top of the thread of what’s happening, I could actually damage client relationships or accounts. And that’s scary, because it’s not that I don’t care. If anything, I care a lot, and then I feel awful when the outcome doesn’t match the effort I thought I was putting in.\n\nSo yes, professionally especially, I do feel like it’s started to affect my ability to maintain the standard I want for myself. Maybe mildly in the sense that I’m still functioning and still working, but it’s noticeable enough now that I can’t just write it off as being busy or having an off week.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant problems in your social relationships because of your restlessness or difficulty staying on track?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not anything I’d call significant.\n\nI mean, I can think of small things. Like, if I’m with friends and my mind drifts for a second, I might miss part of what someone said, or I might interrupt without meaning to because I’m excited or I think I’ll forget what I was going to say. And sometimes I’ll feel embarrassed afterward, like, “Was I being rude?” But I don’t think it has caused major social problems.\n\nI still have friends, I’m still invited places, and I don’t think people are avoiding me because of it. If anything, I’m probably more worried about it internally than other people are reacting to it outwardly. So no, I wouldn’t say it’s significantly damaged my social relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do that more than I’d like to admit.\n\nIt happens a lot in work conversations, especially with clients or coworkers. Someone will still be asking the question, and I’ll already think I know where they’re going, so I jump in with an answer before they’ve actually finished. In my head it feels like I’m being responsive or helpful, like, “Oh, I know this one,” but then sometimes I realize halfway through that I answered the wrong thing because they were actually asking something slightly different.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed because it can come across like I wasn’t listening, or like I cut them off. I’m not trying to be rude — it’s more like the answer is already forming and I almost feel this pressure to get it out before I lose it. But yes, I definitely interrupt or blurt things out sometimes, and then afterward I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just let them finish?”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F90.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Combined Presentation). even if it’s just to refill water or pace by the printer.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: In team pitches I can hear myself going on too long about the campaign idea, and by the time I stop, I can tell other people were waiting for a chance to speak.\n- key: S016\n  name: Blurting_Answers\n  description: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n  manifestation: I often jump in with an answer before a client or coworker has finished asking the question, and then I realize I answered the wrong thing or cut them off.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting my turn on client calls or in office discussions makes me restless, so I keep checking the clock or rehearsing what I want to say until I blurt it out too early.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: If I think of an idea while someone else is working through a deck, I’ll pop over or interrupt their conversation to add my thought, even when it probably could have waited.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: I remember this starting way before high school—my elementary school teachers wrote that I was bright but rushed, talked out of turn, forgot homework, and had a desk stuffed with trust; one client complaint especially made me worry that I could damage accounts even though I care a lot about doing good work.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably affecting me since I changed into advertising sales strategy about two years and two months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 30 / advertising_sales_agent / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S011\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness or difficulty staying on track has significantly impacted your ability to achieve your goals or maintain a certain standard in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s where it’s started to feel more serious for me. For a long time I could kind of compensate, or at least I thought I could. I’d put in extra time, double-check things, try to make myself care enough that it would override the difficulty staying on track. And because I do care a lot about doing good work, I think I told myself it wasn’t really affecting my goals that much.\n\nBut at work, it has started showing up in ways that are harder to brush off. It’s come up in performance reviews — not necessarily as, like, “you’re terrible at your job,” but more in terms of consistency, follow-through, being fully on top of details, staying aligned with client expectations. And that’s been really upsetting because I’m in advertising sales, so so much of the job is about people trusting that I’m listening, that I understand what they need, that I’ll follow through.\n\nThere was one client complaint in particular that really shook me. And sometimes I’ll feel embarrassed afterward, like, “Was I being rude?” But I don’t think it has caused major social problems.\n\nI still have friends, I’m still invited places, and I don’t think people are avoiding me because of it. If anything, I’m probably more worried about it internally than other people are reacting to it outwardly. So no, I wouldn’t say it’s significantly damaged my social relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Like, I’ll tell myself, “Just sit still, it’s not that hard,” and then ten minutes later I’m standing up to refill my water, or I’m walking over by the printer, or kind of pacing for a second and pretending I’m checking something. I’ll even pace a little, like I’m checking something, and then sit back down and hope nobody thought it was weird. It’s not always like I’m consciously thinking, “I need to move right now,” but in those long sales strategy meetings it builds up in this really uncomfortable way. I’ll be sitting there knowing I’m supposed to stay in my seat, trying to look like I’m listening and being normal, and then I just get this strong urge to get up.\n\nSo I’ll do something that seems acceptable, like refill my water even if I don’t really need more water, or I’ll walk over near the printer, or sort of pace for a second like I’m checking on something. It’s honestly the main reason I came in, because I know everyone else can just sit through the meeting, and I keep leaving my seat even though I’m trying not to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what's being discussed during those long sales strategy meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I’ll go in thinking, okay, I’m going to pay attention, I’m going to follow the numbers, I’m going to track what the plan is for this client or campaign. And for the first few minutes I usually can. But then at some point I sort of… drift, I guess.\n\nLike I’ll be looking right at the slide on the screen, or at the brief in front of me, and suddenly realize I’ve been staring at the same chart or the same paragraph and I have no idea what someone just said. It’s not that I don’t care, because I do care, and I know it matters for my job. But it’s like my brain slips off the topic without me noticing, and then I’m trying to piece together the last point from context so I don’t have to ask someone to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you have the urge to move or fidget during those meetings?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, there’s definitely a kind of tension or discomfort when I’m sitting there and I feel like I need to move, but it doesn’t always feel like worry in the classic sense.\n\nSometimes I do get a little self-conscious about it, like I’ll think, “Is everyone noticing that I keep shifting around?” or “Does it look unprofessional if I get up again?” But the main feeling is more like being trapped in my chair, if that makes sense. Like my body is saying I need to get up for a second, and then my mind starts trying to find a socially acceptable excuse to do it.\n\nSo… anxious maybe a little, but I’m not sure that’s the main word I’d use. Restless or uncomfortable feels more accurate.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of restlessness or the need to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. When I look back, it was definitely there way before high school. I remember elementary school being kind of like… I was not a “bad” kid exactly, and I did well enough that people would say I was bright, but the comments from teachers were always these patterns.\n\nThey’d write things like I rushed through work, or I talked out of turn, or I needed to slow down and check my answers. And homework was a whole thing — I’d forget it, or I’d do part of it and then it would disappear into my backpack or my desk. My desk at school was honestly kind of a disaster, just stuffed with half-finished papers and worksheets and things I meant to finish or turn in.\n\nSo, yes, I do remember it starting pretty early. At the time I think everyone just framed it as me needing to try harder or be more organized, but looking back, that restlessness and rushing and not staying on track were already there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying on track consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were less noticeable?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been kind of there in the background for a long time, but if you’re asking when it became noticeably affecting me in a way I couldn’t just brush off, that’s been more since I moved into advertising sales strategy — so about two years and two months ago.\n\nBefore that, I could sort of compensate, or it wasn’t as obvious day to day. But in this role, with the longer strategy discussions and needing to track details over time, it became much harder to ignore. It’s not like it suddenly appeared out of nowhere, but that was when it started feeling like, okay, this is actually getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had relationship problems, or experienced other significant difficulties because of your restlessness or inability to stay on track?\n\nPatient: I haven’t lost a job, no. And I wouldn’t say it’s caused some huge relationship blowup or anything like that. But at work, yes, it’s starting to matter in a way that scares me a little.\n\nIt’s shown up in performance reviews, not like “you’re failing,” but more comments about needing to be more consistent with follow-through, staying on top of details, not letting things slip between conversations and next steps. And in my job, client trust is a big deal, so when I miss something or seem scattered, it’s not just embarrassing, it can actually make the client wonder if I’m on top of their account.\n\nThere was one client complaint in particular that really got to me. I cared a lot about doing good work for them, and I still do, but something about how I handled the tracking or follow-up made them feel like they couldn’t fully rely on me. I remember thinking, “Okay, this isn’t just me being quirky or disorganized in private. This could actually damage an account.”\n\nSo, not catastrophic, but significant enough that I’m worried. It feels like the gap between how much I care and how consistently I execute is becoming visible to other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying on track is more because you have trouble paying attention or because you get distracted by the urge to move or do something else?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the paying attention part, honestly. The movement or wanting to do something else can be there sometimes, but the thing I notice most is that my brain just… slips off the track.\n\nLike in longer strategy meetings, I’ll start out really engaged. I’ll be looking at the deck, taking in what someone is saying, and I genuinely intend to follow the whole conversation. But then a few minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same slide and I don’t actually know what the last point was. It’s not always that I’m physically getting up or needing to move right then. It’s more like I’m present in the room, but I’m not absorbing it.\n\nSame thing when I’m reading a client brief. I can begin focused, and then I’ll catch myself on the same paragraph or the same sentence, and I have no idea what I just read. Sometimes I’ll have to go back and reread it, and even then I’m sort of fighting to keep my attention anchored there.\n\nSo, if I had to choose, I’d say it’s more trouble sustaining attention than being distracted specifically by the urge to move. It feels like my focus just kind of fades out without me noticing until I’ve already missed something.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your restlessness or difficulty staying on track?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I’m sure there are little moments where someone close to me has been annoyed because I seemed distracted or I forgot part of a conversation, but I wouldn’t say it’s caused serious relationship problems.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve had friendships end or a partner say, “This is unbearable,” at least not because of that. If anything, it’s more that I sometimes worry I come across as not fully present, even when I really do care. Like I’ll be listening, and then later realize I didn’t retain as much as I should have, and then I feel bad because I don’t want someone to think I’m dismissing them.\n\nBut overall, no, I don’t think my personal relationships have taken a big hit from it. It feels much more obvious and consequential at work than in my personal life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying on track consistently since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I don’t have a perfect memory of being a little kid, obviously, but this doesn’t feel like something that suddenly started in adulthood.\n\nI remember it being there way before high school. In elementary school, my teachers would write things like I was bright, but I rushed through work, or I talked out of turn, or I forgot to bring in homework even when I had done it. And my desk was always kind of a disaster — like stuffed with half-finished papers and old worksheets and things I meant to finish or turn in but somehow didn’t.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone framed it as a serious issue, because I did well enough academically and I was considered capable. But looking back, the pattern feels really familiar: I’d understand things, I’d want to do well, but the follow-through and keeping track of everything were already messy. So yes, it feels like this has been pretty consistent since childhood, just showing up in different ways now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your restlessness or difficulty staying on track has significantly impacted your ability to achieve your goals or maintain a certain standard in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s where it’s started to feel more serious for me. For a long time I could kind of compensate, or at least I thought I could. I’d put in extra time, double-check things, try to make myself care enough that it would override the difficulty staying on track. And because I do care a lot about doing good work, I think I told myself it wasn’t really affecting my goals that much.\n\nBut at work, it has started showing up in ways that are harder to brush off. It’s come up in performance reviews — not necessarily as, like, “you’re terrible at your job,” but more in terms of consistency, follow-through, being fully on top of details, staying aligned with client expectations. And that’s been really upsetting because I’m in advertising sales, so so much of the job is about people trusting that I’m listening, that I understand what they need, that I’ll follow through.\n\nThere was one client complaint in particular that really shook me. I don’t want to overstate it like I ruined the whole account, but it made me worry that if I miss something or don’t stay on top of the thread of what’s happening, I could actually damage client relationships or accounts. And that’s scary, because it’s not that I don’t care. If anything, I care a lot, and then I feel awful when the outcome doesn’t match the effort I thought I was putting in.\n\nSo yes, professionally especially, I do feel like it’s started to affect my ability to maintain the standard I want for myself. Maybe mildly in the sense that I’m still functioning and still working, but it’s noticeable enough now that I can’t just write it off as being busy or having an off week.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant problems in your social relationships because of your restlessness or difficulty staying on track?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not anything I’d call significant.\n\nI mean, I can think of small things. Like, if I’m with friends and my mind drifts for a second, I might miss part of what someone said, or I might interrupt without meaning to because I’m excited or I think I’ll forget what I was going to say. And sometimes I’ll feel embarrassed afterward, like, “Was I being rude?” But I don’t think it has caused major social problems.\n\nI still have friends, I’m still invited places, and I don’t think people are avoiding me because of it. If anything, I’m probably more worried about it internally than other people are reacting to it outwardly. So no, I wouldn’t say it’s significantly damaged my social relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do that more than I’d like to admit.\n\nIt happens a lot in work conversations, especially with clients or coworkers. Someone will still be asking the question, and I’ll already think I know where they’re going, so I jump in with an answer before they’ve actually finished. In my head it feels like I’m being responsive or helpful, like, “Oh, I know this one,” but then sometimes I realize halfway through that I answered the wrong thing because they were actually asking something slightly different.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed because it can come across like I wasn’t listening, or like I cut them off. I’m not completely sure, honestly. Work is where I notice it the most because there are meetings, deadlines, client calls — like, there are more obvious moments where staying on track is being tested, if that makes sense.
